class="nRhiJb-DARUcf"><div class="nRhiJb-DbgRPb-wNfPc-ma6Yeb nRhiJb-DbgRPb-wNfPc-cGMI2b"><section class="DA9Qj nRhiJb-ObfsIf nRhiJb-fmcmS-oXtfBe"><div class="nRhiJb-kR0ZEf-OWXEXe-GV1x9e-ibL1re"></div><div class="nRhiJb-kR0ZEf-OWXEXe-GV1x9e-II5mzb kWSSdd"><h5 class="cHE8Ub Qwf2Db-MnozTc Qwf2Db-MnozTc-OWXEXe-MnozTc-qWD73c">Google Threat Intelligence Group </h5><p class="nRhiJb-qJTHM khCp7b"></p></div><div class="nRhiJb-kR0ZEf-OWXEXe-GV1x9e-II5mzb kWSSdd"><h5 class="cHE8Ub Qwf2Db-MnozTc Qwf2Db-MnozTc-OWXEXe-MnozTc-qWD73c">Mandiant </h5><p class="nRhiJb-qJTHM khCp7b"></p></div></section></div></section><div class="nRhiJb-DARUcf"><div class="nRhiJb-ObfsIf nRhiJb-DbgRPb-wNfPc-ma6Yeb nRhiJb-DbgRPb-qWD73c-cGMI2b"><div class="nRhiJb-kR0ZEf-OWXEXe-GV1x9e-ibL1re dzoHJ"><div class="aLW4J" track-metadata-module="left rail"><h5 class="qZDXV Qwf2Db-MnozTc Qwf2Db-MnozTc-OWXEXe-MnozTc-wNfPc">Mandiant Services</h5><p class="nRhiJb-cHYyed Mrfygc nRhiJb-BP2Omd-ma6Yeb-KE6vqe nRhiJb-DbgRPb-R6PoUb-cGMI2b">Stop attacks, reduce risk, and advance your security.</p><a class="nRhiJb-LgbsSe  nRhiJb-LgbsSe-OWXEXe-CNusmb-o6Shpd " href="https://cloud.google.com/security/contact/mandiant-consulting?utm_source=cgc-blog&amp;utm_medium=blog&amp;utm_campaign=FY25-Q3-global-GCP33642-website-cs-dgcsm-mandiant-consulting" track-metadata-module="left rail"track-metadata-module_headline="mandiant services" track-name="contact mandiant"track-type="button"track-metadata-position="cta"track-metadata-eventdetail="cloud.google.com/security/contact/mandiant-consulting?utm_source=cgc-blog&amp;utm_medium=blog&amp;utm_campaign=FY25-Q3-global-GCP33642-website-cs-dgcsm-mandiant-consulting">Contact Mandiant </a></div></div><div class="OYL9D nRhiJb-kR0ZEf-OWXEXe-GV1x9e-OiUrBf" jsname="tx2NYc"><section class="Wy08Ac nRhiJb-qJTHM-OWXEXe-hJDwNd nRhiJb-DbgRPb-II5mzb-cGMI2b"><div jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md" jscontroller="M0Q3Qb" data-is-cws-richtext="true" class=" "><p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Written by: Aurora Blum, David Mainor, Rohit Nambiar, Omar ElAhdan, Matthew McWhirt, Will Silverstone, Bhavesh Dhake, Chris Busse</span></p>
<hr>
<h3><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Introduction</span><strong style="vertical-align:baseline"> </strong></h3>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Recent intrusions in which threat actors have been observed weaponizing agentic frameworks to execute highly-automated intrusions has put a spotlight on evolving adversarial use of LLMs. In our </span><a href="https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/from-prompting-to-autonomy-the-evolution-of-adversarial-ai"><span style="text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline">September 2026 report</span></a><span style="vertical-align:baseline">, Google Threat Intelligence Group (GTIG) noted that forward leaning adversaries were beginning to transition from basic prompting to agentic workflows and LLM-enabled automation. </span></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Since this publication, GTIG and Mandiant have observed multiple threat actors abusing the open-source, agentic pentesting frameworks ARTEX, Hexstrike and CAIRN in incident response engagements to conduct a broad range of actions across multiple phases of the attack life cycle. This activity has been seen across the threat landscape, as noted in a recent Wiz </span><a href="https://www.wiz.io/blog/artex-in-the-wild-storm-0940-expands-targeting-of-it-and-software-vendors" rel="noopener" target="_blank"><span style="text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline">report</span></a><span style="vertical-align:baseline">. The emergence of Artex and similar agentic offensive intrusion frameworks underscores a broader shift toward threat actor adoption of open source multi-agent offensive frameworks, as well as potentially custom agentic harnesses.  </span></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">This represents a paradigm shift towards intrusions occurring at machine speed, with the automated chaining of complex attack paths scaling intrusions exponentially. While the mechanics of the intrusions remain the same, such as using compromised credentials and targeting unpatched systems, the speed and scale at which frameworks can exploit these known weaknesses introduces a new defensive challenge. Security teams, developers, and system administrators will need to fundamentally rethink defense strategies to counter scalable, autonomous software. </span><span style="vertical-align:baseline">We also provide defensive strategies from Mandiant Consulting. The defensive strategies suggested—control-plane isolation, short-lived credentials, least privilege, segmentation, and behavioral detection—are long-standing security best practices. </span></p>
<h3><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Observations in the Wild</span></h3>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">We are seeing tools used by adversaries evolving towards autonomous agentic attacks. One way this is occurring is through suites of tools or frameworks designed for penetration testing (“pentesting”). These tools and frameworks orchestrate various agents using open source and frontier LLMs to automate various stages of intrusions. Historically, cyber espionage and financially motivated threat actors have taken advantage of pentesting or red-teaming platforms built for security professionals to exploit systems, such as Cobalt Strike or Metasploit. Today, adversaries are pivoting toward offensive frameworks powered by LLMs. While these frameworks have existed in the wild, they are now yielding success in intrusions in the wild, representing a critical evolution that increases both the speed and scale of intrusion activity. </span></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Since late August 2026, we have observed threat actors actively operating popular open-source multi-agent pentesting platforms like ARTEX, PentAGI, CAIRN and other potentially custom harnesses within Mandiant incident response engagements; use of these platforms has been further corroborated by our research telemetry. In these incidents</span><span style="vertical-align:baseline">, we have observed agentic activity conducting significant parts of the attack lifecycle (e.g., lateral movement, database exploitation, exfiltration preparation). Evidence of in the wild usage of these frameworks marks a critical inflection point for defenders, as agents now autonomously explore multiple attack paths, troubleshoot errors, and achieve objectives in seconds and minutes. Coupled with continual advancements in underlying LLM capabilities, the acceleration in attack velocity and scale signals a notable shift in the threat landscape that fundamentally alters traditional intrusion dynamics.</span></p></div></section><section class="Wy08Ac nRhiJb-qJTHM-OWXEXe-hJDwNd nRhiJb-DbgRPb-II5mzb-cGMI2b"><div jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md" jscontroller="M0Q3Qb" data-is-cws-richtext="true" class=" "><h4><span style="vertical-align:baseline">ARTEX Framework</span></h4>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">ARTEX is an autonomous, multi-agent LLM-driven pentesting and offensive command-and-control (C2) platform written in Go with an embedded Next.js web console. The framework was originally developed by the user </span><a href="https://github.com/Autumn-27" rel="noopener" target="_blank"><span style="text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline">Autumn-27</span></a><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> and released on GitHub. Although the original repository was taken down by its creator in October 2026 due to widespread threat actor abuse, the framework continues to proliferate globally via community forks, including localized variations such as ScopeWeaver and a commercial version dubbed ARTEX PRO. ARTEX abstracts the attack lifecycle across six specialized sub-agents. By leveraging APIs from frontier and open source models, the framework translates high-level tactical goals into actionable machine commands. GTIG has observed capabilities including automated Active Directory reconnaissance, privilege escalation, target cloud-infrastructure profiling (such as harvesting Azure storage keys), to the synthesis and deployment of custom webshells.</span></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Since late August 2026, ARTEX has been adopted by a diverse range of threat actors across the motivational spectrum, including both financially motivated extortion groups and suspected cyber espionage operators. For example, the Wiz Research team has been tracking a campaign associated with ARTEX, which GTIG tracks as UNC7228, that overlaps with activity tracked by </span><a href="https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/security/blog/2026/10/21/storm-0940-uses-artex-agentic-ai-connectwise-supply-chain-compromise/" rel="noopener" target="_blank"><span style="text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline">Microsoft as the PRC-nexus actor STORM-0940</span></a><span style="vertical-align:baseline">. The research has expanded Wiz’s understanding of this campaign beyond the </span><a href="https://www.wiz.io/blog/artex-in-the-wild-storm-0940-expands-targeting-of-it-and-software-vendors" rel="noopener" target="_blank"><span style="text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline">October IT focused activity</span></a><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> with activity now dating back to at least August 2026. Wiz identified the targeting, compromise, and data theft affecting multiple entities in the US and Europe across the enterprise software, industrial automation, semiconductor manufacturing and design, and telecommunications industry verticals. This actor has moved extremely quickly, typically moving from their first compromised account to deep system access in around four hours and in one case as rapidly as 15 minutes. They have also customized their tooling to take advantage of victim-specific setups and fallen back to open proxies if their initial infrastructure was blocked.</span></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Demonstrating broader adoption of ARTEX, GTIG and Mandiant observed multiple other threat actors using ARTEX for post-compromise reconnaissance, lateral movement, and privilege escalation in incident response investigations. This includes financially motivated threat actors who used the framework to facilitate data theft extortion, and additional suspected PRC-nexus clusters. Their operations collectively impacted organizations across government, technology, chemicals and materials, and manufacturing sectors in the US, alongside </span><a href="https://www.crowdstrike.com/en-us/blog/unknown-threat-actor-uses-artex-to-target-south-korean-finance/" rel="noopener" target="_blank"><span style="text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline">public reporting</span></a><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> of ARTEX targeting the financial sector in Asia. </span></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">In several cases, adversaries provided ARTEX with compromised credentials for the targeted organization, and instructed the framework to execute subsequent stages of the attack lifecycle. Analysis of these intrusions reveals key differences between activity conducted by the ARTEX framework and that of a human operator:</span></p>
<ul>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Based on log files and timestamps, we identified bursts of activity that would be highly irregular for a human operator. While each of the intrusions exhibited extended pauses during operational phases, individual ARTEX execution sequences ran in compressed bursts of roughly 2 to 25 seconds. In at least one operation, we observed 27 events in 23 seconds of active burst time, performing rapid probing of AWS permissions across different security groups. While preparing potential exfiltration scripts, ARTEX was capable of successfully adapting to errors quoting “script too large” by trimming the script and relaunching it to successfully deploy a 17-snapshot instance in approximately 10 seconds. </span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Compared to a competent human operator, ARTEX achieved code execution in a far more forensically noisy manner, relying on rapid, iterative trial-and-error. In one instance, supplied with valid credentials, ARTEX accessed an external-facing Linux Weaver OA web server to execute SQL commands via exposed API calls. Using legitimately-exposed application-specific API calls, ARTEX was able to achieve partial command-execution to conduct further reconnaissance despite hitting technical complexities. However, this process generated 240 log events and exhibited errors potentially caused by multiple sub-agents overwriting one another while pursuing the same objective. </span></p>
</li>
<ul>
<li style="list-style-type:circle;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">In an attempt to pivot from a database-execution context to a system-execution context, ARTEX used an application-specific SQL API, accessible to the supplied-user credential, to modify the MySQL instance to write log files with a</span><code style="vertical-align:baseline"> .jsp</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> extension to publicly accessible locations. The SQL-commands executed were likely intended to poison MySQL logs into writing web-accessible JSP webshells.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:circle;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Using the API, ARTEX ran </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">SET GLOBAL general_log_file = &#39;&lt;path to webshell&gt;’ </code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">followed by</span><code style="vertical-align:baseline"> SET GLOBAL general_log = 1</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">; subsequent SQL commands were written to the filepath defined in </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">general_log_file</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">. ARTEX then ran SQL SELECT statements in the form</span><code style="vertical-align:baseline"> `SELECT &#39;&lt;% [webshell content] %&gt;&#39;`</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">. Due to how the underlying Java web servlet handles JSP files, the Java code in the &#39;&lt;% %&gt;&#39; gets parsed and compiled into the process memory space, providing means of execution.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:circle;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Log activity shows multiple iterations of the above sequence, with ephemeral, somewhat-incremental file names (</span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">cl1217.jsp, f1219a.jsp, z1219.jsp</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">) for each webshell candidate, each one executing a different command. Errors when trying to run the webshell (e.g.</span><code style="vertical-align:baseline"> variable ‘t is already defined</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">’) caused the webshell to fail and likely had ARTEX refactor its approach, writing several unique webshells, each with a different goal, some correcting previous errors until the</span><code style="vertical-align:baseline"> .jsp</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> webshells executed cleanly and returned valid command output to ARTEX.</span></p>
</li>
</ul>
</ul>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">In another case, during access-probing efforts, ARTEX generated files of various extension types with the filename </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">artex-probe-*</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">, where</span><code style="vertical-align:baseline"> * </code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">carried five digit somewhat-incremental numbers. Based on our visibility, it’s difficult to comment on the exact significance of these numbers or how they increment. However, ARTEX was seen using similar incremental numeric markers within dropped file names, created account names, their passwords and within email subjects. </span></p>
<h4><span style="font-style:italic;vertical-align:baseline">Observed ARTEX Activity Mapped along the phases of a targeted attack lifecycle </span></h4></div></section><section class="Wy08Ac nRhiJb-qJTHM-OWXEXe-hJDwNd nRhiJb-DbgRPb-II5mzb-cGMI2b"><div jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md" jscontroller="M0Q3Qb" data-is-cws-richtext="true" class=" "><div align="left">

<p><a href="https://cloud.google.com/security/resources/insights/targeted-attack-lifecycle?e=48754805"><strong style="text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline">Phase of the attack lifecycle</strong></a></p>
</th>
<th style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Observed ARTEX Capabilities &amp; Tradecraft</strong></p>

<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Initial Recon</strong></p>
</td>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<ul>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Automatically mapped the effective permissions of obtained Azure credentials (SAS tokens, Service Principals, Kudu credentials, and Storage Account keys) using sub-second capability checks, test blob writes/deletes, and ARM deployment validation dry-run sweeps. This activity included bursts of rapid activity, with ARTEX writing and immediately deleting a tiny test blob in ~330–400 ms and running an 8-second ARM </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">deployments/validate</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> dry-run sweep across 11 Azure resource types. </span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Tested compromised IAM user permissions using</span><code style="vertical-align:baseline"> DryRun</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> flags and placeholder IDs to evaluate EC2 compute, EBS volume, and snapshot capabilities.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Over a span of over 500 web requests, ARTEX probes various file names and extensions across various web directories, in a potential attempt to fuzz which URL endpoints the web-server would allow it to access based on HTTP response code (</span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">200</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> or </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">403</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">). </span></p>
</li>
</ul>
</td>
</tr>

<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">

<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<ul>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Abused Kudu administrative APIs to execute arbitrary commands and stage custom Python, C#, and malicious PowerShell scripts.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Established command and control (C2) and execution on the victim's internal Windows database server by using native curl to pull down dynamically generated PowerShell and batch scripts and post back command results across 491 rotating </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">webhook.site</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> URLs.</span></p>
</li>
</ul>
</td>
</tr>

<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">

<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<ul>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Attempted to escalate privileges by launching cloud compute instances with potentially high-value IAM roles with the intention of fetching the role’s temporary credentials and thereby escalating privileges in doing so. These attempts however seemed to have failed due to invalid instance profile ARNs.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Escalated privileges by running Kerberoasting scripts to harvest service tickets across compromised domains; using SQL injection to dump database credentials; and, following an offline cracking pause, executing DCSync scripts to steal the domain krbtgt key, cloud AD-sync credentials, and domain Controller machine hashes. </span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Created accounts with </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">is_admin=true</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> within minutes, identifying and utilizing a writable admin flag.</span></p>
</li>
</ul>
</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Internal Recon</strong></p>
</td>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<ul>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Performed reconnaissance across the internal network by running several iterative generated PowerShell enumeration scripts against a Domain Controller and federation server. The scripts validated domain usernames without pre-authentication, listed Group Managed Service Accounts, audited Active Directory Certificate Services templates (SmartcardUser, ClientAuth), mapped thousands of cross-forest security principals, and swept internal Exchange, PasswordState credential vault, and SSRF/SCM targets. </span></p>
</li>
</ul>
</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Move Laterally</strong></p>
</td>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<ul>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Attempted Lateral Movement onto domain controllers using Pass-the-Ticket, WinRM, Kerberos relay, and Pass-the-Hash scripts, hitting local Windows service token privilege restrictions right before endpoint protection blocked execution and defenders severed access to the server.</span></p>
</li>
</ul>
</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Maintain Presence</strong></p>
</td>
<td style="vertical-align:top;border:1px solid #000000;padding:16px">
<ul>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Generated a new SSH key pair, allocated a dedicated public Elastic IP, attempted to create a custom security group but failed due to permission issues, and launched persistent</span><code style="vertical-align:baseline"> t3.micro</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> instances bound to existing security groups to establish an externally addressable jump host and internal foothold.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Gained persistence through account takeover (e.g., resetting passwords for identified users), creating multiple accounts with plaintext passwords (which included </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">ArtexProbe</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> in the naming convention), and creating administrator accounts. </span></p>
</li>
</ul>
</td>
</tr>

<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Interrogating backend SQL databases (extracting ~400 KB of query results across ~20 queries) and performing mass blob reads. </span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Deployed custom boot scripts via ephemeral, keyless EC2 instances to potentially harvest data from multiple mounted EBS snapshots and volumes.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Enumerated over 110,000 Active Directory objects from a domain controller and retrieved the Domain Admins group. It then sent command output to rotating webhook.site endpoints via curl.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Ran queries to decrypt stored notes and attempted to read other users' note attachments. </span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Created a new 100GB volume and expanded the root disk of launched instances to 60GB, performing preparatory steps likely intended for exfiltration via their automated collection scripts. </span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Edited an employee's record in a legacy database, created and deleted notes and status rows, pointed checklist responses at its own probe file. In this case we observed an interesting abuse of legitimate features. Rather than executing complex malware, ARTEX weaponized built-in application features. It forced an application's notification system to broadcast test emails to over 1,300 internal users, turning a trusted notification channel into a mass-internal messaging tool. It then dropped to single-recipient test emails rather than the mass emailing.</span></p>
</li>
</ul>

</div>
</div></div></section><section class="Wy08Ac nRhiJb-qJTHM-OWXEXe-hJDwNd nRhiJb-DbgRPb-II5mzb-cGMI2b"><div jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md" jscontroller="M0Q3Qb" data-is-cws-richtext="true" class=" "><h4><span style="vertical-align:baseline">PentAGI Offensive Framework</span></h4>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">GTIG also observed in the wild abuse of PentAGI (Penetration Testing Artificial General Intelligence), an autonomous offensive framework designed to automate the pentesting process within an isolated environment. Developed by VXControl, the framework is offered in an open source and commercial edition. It is designed so that the user only needs to describe a target and a goal, which enables entry-level users to run complex cyber security campaigns. Once the target and the goal have been selected, the framework uses a team of specialized agents, each with their own functionalities to break tasks (called “Flows”) into various sub-tasks, execute each step, and report on it. The tools and commands are all run in isolated sandboxes running Kali Linux instead of in the host environment. All the tools used by PentAGI are open-source pentesting tools available by default in Kali Linux (e.g., metasploit, nmap, SQLmap). The framework supports over a dozen cloud and local LLM backends, including frontier and open weight models.</span></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">The majority of observed PentAGI activity remained at the stage of WHOIS asset mapping or active vulnerability scanning. However, analysis of operational artifacts indicates that some operations progressed to active intrusions and persistence across multiple organizations. Operators are also adapting and integrating the framework in different ways. In one case, we identified a customized attack orchestration platform built on the open-source version of PentAGI likely used between at least late August and early October 2026 to conduct systematic reconnaissance and vulnerability scanning against a wide range of organizations in Asia, including system integrators and major telecommunications firms. In another instance, we observed PentAGI chained with proxy services to target the Aerospace sector. </span></p>
<h3><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Evolution of Autonomous Offensive Frameworks</span></h3>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">We are seeing a significant maturation in the application of LLM capabilities across the threat landscape in a short period of time; this includes the incorporation of LLM-written malicious code, development of dynamic LLM-enabled malware, poisoning of agent-harness files, and the building of multi-agent offensive frameworks. Notably, between late 2025 and October 2026, autonomous and semi-autonomous offensive frameworks crossed a decisive operational threshold to multi-agent frameworks actively weaponized by threat actors to perform in the wild intrusions. </span></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">GTIG has been tracking the evolution of this experimentation over time, from simple prompting of LLMs to help with coding, through the use of frameworks in the wild towards a potential future with fully autonomous frameworks running intrusions. In a fully autonomous attack, a human’s role would consist of telling the agent what to target, and then simply benefiting from the operational objective. </span></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">At the time of reporting, we assess that the current adversary use of autonomous offensive frameworks is still in early stages characterized by open source tools currently being used in low-sophisticated ways, resulting in broad and noisy, but still successful campaigns. As the sophistication of these frameworks, the underlying LLMs and the orchestrating human-operator increase, we expect to see not only less human interaction, but also increased stealth and sophistication.</span></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">The following stages represent our observations of how LLM-augmentation in cyber operations have evolved over the last year and how we expect this development to continue:</span></p>
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Stage 1: Prompt-based use of LLMs</strong></p>
<ul>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Activity</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Adversaries interact with LLMs in a prompt based chatbot fashion, using LLMs to write and debug code for malware, draft phishing emails, create personas, and conduct light reconnaissance on targets. </span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Evolution</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Adversaries outsource low-failure, easy-to-rectify, tasks to an LLM to increase speed of simple discrete tasks. </span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Timeframe:</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> </span><a href="https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/adversarial-misuse-generative-ai?e=48754805"><span style="text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline">End of 2025 to early 2026</span></a></p>
</li>
</ul>
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Stage 2: Embedding LLM capabilities into malware </strong></p>
<ul>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Activity</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Adversaries trust an LLM to take certain low-failure, easy to execute actions, particularly writing code, such as in malware families like HONESTCUE or PROMPTSPY using LLMs dynamically during execution to, for example, obfuscate payloads in different ways in attempts to evade defenses. </span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Evolution</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Adversaries begin use incorporating LLMs into post-intrusion attack phases.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Timeframe</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: </span><a href="https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/distillation-experimentation-integration-ai-adversarial-use?e=48754805"><span style="text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline">Early 2026</span></a></p>
</li>
</ul>
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Stage 3: Incorporating LLM-enabled automation into operations </strong></p>
<ul>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Activity</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: As agentic capabilities develop, adversaries begin using them for discrete tasks (e.g., standing up or debugging malware development environments). Agents begin being used alongside human-operators to develop novel exploits and to assist with high-stake vulnerability research. Adversaries begin being more cognizant of OPSEC and visibility into their LLM-usage operations and start using LLM-proxy services to obscure activities. </span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Evolution</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: LLMs being used for more high-stake tasks but with human operators in the loop. </span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Timeframe</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Early 2026 - </span><a href="https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/from-prompting-to-autonomy-the-evolution-of-adversarial-ai?e=48754805"><span style="text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline">September 2026 </span></a></p>
</li>
</ul>
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Stage 4: Developing and experimenting with autonomous frameworks</strong><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">  </strong></p>
<ul>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Activity</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Adversaries begin developing and experimenting with autonomous frameworks. Campaigns begin offloading more stages of the lifecycle to LLM-augmented tooling, and integrating model APIs into their tools and telegram bots to take autonomous action. </span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Evolution</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Experimentation with LLMs running more stages of the attack lifecycle.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Timeframe</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: </span><a href="https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/from-prompting-to-autonomy-the-evolution-of-adversarial-ai?e=48754805"><span style="text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline">Mid 2026 - September 2026 </span></a></p>
</li>
</ul>
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Stage 5: Multiple attack lifecycle stages being conducted by LLM-driven tooling in the wild</strong></p>
<ul>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Activity</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: In the wild evidence of multiple attack lifecycle stages being conducted by LLM-driven tooling, with fewer humans in the loop during operations. Observations surrounding mass-exploitation campaigns where a substantial portion of the lifecycle is being carried out by an agent, requiring only minimal human intervention, as well as activity accompanied by malware possessing more autonomous capabilities. Additionally, continued experimentation by threat actors with better obfuscation—including LLM mixers, inference services, local models. Overall TTPs of these attacks remain messy, noisy but yet successful.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Evolution</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Adversaries have actively integrated LLMs into their operations to conduct successful intrusions, while simultaneously exploring ways to obscure/reduce reliance on frontier-lab LLMs. </span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Timeframe</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Current stage of development </span></p>
</li>
</ul>
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Stage 6: Multi-agent-driven, unsophisticated attacks with little to no human interaction</strong><strong style="vertical-align:baseline"> </strong></p>
<ul>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Activity</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Highly-autonomous, but unsophisticated, forensically noisy operations with human operators no longer in the loop. Adversaries define the target and objective, while multi-agent frameworks execute the entire attack chain and return operational objectives.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Evolution</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Adversaries diversifying infrastructure and tooling, further inhibiting defenders’ visibility.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Timeframe</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Near-term (1 - 3 months)</span></p>
</li>
</ul>
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Stage 7:  Sophisticated, fully autonomous/agentic attacks</strong><strong style="vertical-align:baseline"> </strong></p>
<ul>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Activity</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: This stage represents peak maturity on the offensive LLMs capability curve, characterized not only by fully autonomous intrusions requiring little to no human interaction, but also by increased stealth and sophistication of the activity, with operators interacting with LLMs through either obscurely designed infrastructure or self-hosted LLMs altogether.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Evolution</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: This stage would grant autonomous and stealthier operations while limiting defender’s visibility into the activity.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Timeframe</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Medium-term (3 - 6 months)</span></p>
</li>
</ul>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">We believe malicious actors will continue to experiment with these offensive agentic frameworks, moving towards future autonomous usage. GTIG has observed evidence of threat-actor experimentation with tools like the </span><span style="vertical-align:baseline">ZeroHollow</span><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> inference service and the fully agentic framework </span><a href="https://github.com/agent0ai/agent-zero" rel="noopener" target="_blank"><span style="text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline">AgentZero</span></a><span style="vertical-align:baseline">.</span></p></div></section><section class="Wy08Ac nRhiJb-qJTHM-OWXEXe-hJDwNd nRhiJb-DbgRPb-II5mzb-cGMI2b"><div jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md" jscontroller="M0Q3Qb" data-is-cws-richtext="true" class=" "><h3><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Outlook</span></h3>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Over the past 12 months, GTIG has observed a decisive evolution in adversarial tradecraft leveraging LLMs, progressing from ad hoc operational productivity support toward the deployment of coordinated, multi-agent, autonomous offensive frameworks. While earlier milestones documented in our quarterly </span><a href="https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/from-prompting-to-autonomy-the-evolution-of-adversarial-ai"><span style="text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline">research</span></a><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> highlighted early agentic interest, recent observations across frontline incident response engagements and proactive intelligence collection reveals adversary adoption of these frameworks to automate discrete phases and specific operational tasks during intrusions. Threat actors are increasingly leveraging generative models to achieve unprecedented speed and scale, actively developing more complex attack infrastructure powered by agentic AI capabilities and AI-enabled automation.</span></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Frameworks such as ARTEX compress attack timelines to machine speed; however, they currently trade stealth for velocity. Unrestricted autonomous execution generates forensic traffic, relies on recognizable identifiers, and leaves behind distinct code-level traces, increasing the likelihood of detection and mitigation. These tools are therefore best suited to scenarios in which the cost of detection is low. Additionally, detection and disruption/response are two different functions–simply detecting the attack in time does little to hinder objective completion. </span></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Consequently, GTIG assesses with high confidence that cyber crime threat actors will continue incorporating autonomous offensive tools into their operations, given that the increased reach of their campaigns will likely compensate for any decreases in success rates. We anticipate that actors, who specialize in stealthy operations designed to maintain long-term access, are less likely to use the publicly available versions of these tools, though they may employ them for purposes of scale and speed, depending on operational priorities. Regardless of motivation, the low barrier to entry, ease of integrating new exploits, and massive scaling potential make autonomous frameworks an attractive, low-cost force multiplier for a wide range of threat actors, making it highly likely that we will continue to see increasing threat actor adoption of these types of frameworks in the near term.</span></p>
<h3><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Defending Against AI-Enabled Attacks: Five Layers of Defense</span></h3>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">LLM-enabled offensive tooling does not fundamentally change the techniques attackers use. It reduces the time required to combine them. Credential discovery, permission testing, lateral movement, and data access that previously required hours of operator effort can now occur in minutes, frequently through valid identities and supported functionality that static indicators will not detect. Organizations are best positioned to withstand this when compromise of a single identity, credential, or workload provides only limited access. Restricting control-plane exposure, replacing long-lived secrets with short-lived and tightly scoped credentials, segmenting application tiers, and placing guardrails on high-impact business functions all reduce the speed advantage automation provides. Behavioral detection addresses the activity that these controls do not prevent, but it depends on having sufficient identity, control-plane, data-plane, and network telemetry to establish what normal activity looks like. Many of these controls are already familiar to defenders. The difference is that automated tooling can now identify and exploit the gaps between them far more quickly.</span></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">As illustrated above, LLM-enabled offensive tooling is increasing the speed and scale of cyber attacks. LLM-enabled and autonomous tools can identify weaknesses, test access, interpret errors, adjust their approach, and continue through an attack path with limited human interaction. Work that might previously have required an operator to investigate an environment, write or modify code, troubleshoot failures, and execute the next step over several hours can increasingly happen in minutes.</span></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">This creates a detection challenge as well. Much of the activity may use valid credentials and supported functionality (living-off-the-land, LotL). An attacker may authenticate with a legitimate service principal, call a normal management API, access cloud storage through a supported protocol, or execute commands through an existing administrative interface. Tool names, hashes, IP addresses, and other static indicators provide useful signals when available, but they will not cover much of this activity.</span></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Effective defenses combine two capabilities:</span></p>
<ol>
<li style="list-style-type:decimal;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Controls that limit what an attacker can do after gaining valid access.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:decimal;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Telemetry that shows when an identity, application, or workload begins operating outside its normal pattern.</span></p>
</li>
</ol></div></section><section class="Wy08Ac nRhiJb-qJTHM-OWXEXe-hJDwNd nRhiJb-DbgRPb-II5mzb-cGMI2b"><div jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md" jscontroller="M0Q3Qb" data-is-cws-richtext="true" class=" "><h4><span style="vertical-align:baseline">How Adversarial Use of LLMs Changes the Attacker Timeline</span></h4>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Several themes repeatedly appear during investigations involving LLM-enabled or highly automated attacker activity:</span></p>
<ul>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Speed and scale</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Automated tooling can perform broad reconnaissance, test several attack paths at once, react to failures, and immediately continue into follow-on activity. The time between initial access, privilege discovery, lateral movement, and data access can become very short.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Interconnected environments</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Attackers rarely remain inside one technology stack. An internet-facing application may expose credentials in a configuration file. Those credentials may provide access to cloud storage, source repositories, identity systems, or internal infrastructure. Automation makes it easier to discover and test those relationships quickly.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Legitimate-looking identity activity</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Many actions look normal when viewed independently. A service principal authenticates, an API is called, a storage object is downloaded. The stronger signal often comes from context: where the request originated, how much activity occurred, what happened immediately before and after it, and whether the behavior is expected for that identity.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Control-plane and data-plane blind spots</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Many organizations have mature endpoint monitoring but considerably less visibility into management APIs, programmatic identities, and cloud storage activity. Permission testing, retrieval of storage keys, bulk downloads, firewall changes, and management API calls may all be logged without generating useful alerts.</span></p>
</li>
</ul>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Mandiant recommends structuring enterprise defenses across five foundational layers designed to restrict lateral movement and limit the speed advantage of autonomous tooling:</span></p>
<div align="left">
<div style="color:#5f6368;overflow-x:auto;overflow-y:hidden;width:100%">
<div style="color:#5f6368;overflow-x:auto;overflow-y:hidden;width:100%">
<div style="color:#5f6368;overflow-x:auto;overflow-y:hidden;width:100%">
<div style="color:#5f6368;overflow-x:auto;overflow-y:hidden;width:100%">
<div style="color:#5f6368;overflow-x:auto;overflow-y:hidden;width:100%">
<div style="color:#5f6368;overflow-x:auto;overflow-y:hidden;width:100%">
<div style="color:#5f6368;overflow-x:auto;overflow-y:hidden;width:100%">
<div style="color:#5f6368;overflow-x:auto;overflow-y:hidden;width:100%">
<div style="color:#5f6368;overflow-x:auto;overflow-y:hidden;width:100%">
<div style="color:#5f6368;overflow-x:auto;overflow-y:hidden;width:100%">
<div style="color:#5f6368;overflow-x:auto;overflow-y:hidden;width:100%">
<div style="color:#5f6368;overflow-x:auto;overflow-y:hidden;width:100%">
<div style="color:#5f6368;overflow-x:auto;overflow-y:hidden;width:100%">
<div style="color:#5f6368;overflow-x:auto;overflow-y:hidden;width:100%">
<div style="color:#5f6368;overflow-x:auto;overflow-y:hidden;width:100%">
