</div>
<p style="text-align:center"><span style="vertical-align:baseline;color:#5f6368;display:block;font-size:16px;font-style:italic;margin-top:8px;width:100%">Table 1: Foundational layers for restricting adversarial LLM speed advantage and lateral movement</span></p></div></section><section class="Wy08Ac nRhiJb-qJTHM-OWXEXe-hJDwNd nRhiJb-DbgRPb-II5mzb-cGMI2b"><div jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md" jscontroller="M0Q3Qb" data-is-cws-richtext="true" class=" "><h3><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Layer 1: Protect the Control Plane</span></h3>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Restrict access to the systems attackers can use to administer, deploy, or reconfigure the environment.</span></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">The control plane extends well beyond Active Directory or a cloud console. It includes identity providers, cloud management APIs, network and firewall management platforms, virtualization infrastructure, code repositories, CI/CD systems, privileged access management platforms, backup infrastructure, certificate authorities, SaaS administration consoles, and application administration interfaces.</span></p>
<h4><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Why It Is Important</span></h4>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Internet-accessible management interfaces can quickly become part of an attack path after credentials are obtained. For example, Azure App Service includes an administrative interface through Kudu Site Control Manager, or SCM, which supports application deployment and management functionality. If an attacker obtains publishing credentials or another identity capable of accessing that interface, automation can quickly test authentication, enumerate functionality, interact with application files, execute commands, retrieve output, and remove staged artifacts.</span></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Similar exposure exists across firewall administration, virtualization consoles, cloud management APIs, backup platforms, CI/CD interfaces, and other management services. Any reachable administrative path gives an automated attacker another capability to test.</span></p>
<h4><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Mitigations</span></h4>
<ul>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Reduce Administrative Internet Exposure</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Restrict cloud administration, network device management, application publishing interfaces, hypervisors, backup platforms, and other sensitive services to trusted networks, private connectivity, or dedicated administrative paths wherever possible.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Require Trusted Administrative Paths</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Require privileged users to administer sensitive infrastructure from managed devices, privileged access workstations, or approved jump hosts. Pair these controls with phishing-resistant authentication such as FIDO2 or passkeys where supported.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Harden Application Management Interfaces</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Treat application administration and publishing endpoints as privileged infrastructure rather than extensions of the public application.</span></p>
</li>
<ul>
<li style="list-style-type:circle;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Configure access restrictions for Kudu/SCM endpoints separately where appropriate.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:circle;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Limit SCM access to approved management networks or deployment infrastructure.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:circle;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Disable basic publishing authentication where it is not required.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:circle;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Restrict access to deployment and publishing credentials.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:circle;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Consider deployment models such as run-from-package that reduce direct modification of deployed application files.</span></p>
</li>
</ul>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Control Public Exposure by Design</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Establish governance around public IP allocation and require architecture review before workloads or management interfaces are exposed directly to the internet.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Apply Similar Controls to Programmatic Administration</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Where supported, restrict administrative service principals and automation identities to expected networks, workloads, or private connectivity.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Monitor the Control Plane as a Distinct Security Surface</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Centralize authentication, configuration change, deployment, and administrative telemetry from cloud platforms, application administration interfaces, network infrastructure, backup platforms, and other management systems. Alert on administrative access from previously unseen networks, hosting providers, or geographies.</span></p>
</li>
</ul>
<h3><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Layer 2: Restrict Identities and Keys</span></h3>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Reduce the value of stolen credentials by limiting their lifetime, permissions, and where they can be used.</span></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Security programs have traditionally placed more emphasis on human identities. Service principals, service accounts, API keys, storage keys, cloud access keys, deployment tokens, personal access tokens, and application secrets can receive less scrutiny despite providing significant access.</span></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Programmatic identities are especially useful to an automated attacker when they combine long-lived credentials, broad permissions, limited conditional controls, and limited monitoring.</span></p>
<h4><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Why It Is Important</span></h4>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Consider an exposed cloud storage credential. Once automated tooling discovers the credential in source code, an environment variable, application configuration, a database, or another compromised system, it can immediately test the credential and begin enumerating available data.</span></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">A long-lived credential that works from anywhere on the internet and provides broad storage access can turn a single credential exposure into large-scale data access very quickly.</span></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">The same pattern applies to service principals and other programmatic identities. A credential intended for an internal CI/CD runner carries considerably more risk when the identity can also authenticate from an arbitrary network  and access production resources.</span></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Credential security therefore needs to address both what an identity can access and the conditions under which that access is valid.</span></p>
<h4><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Mitigations</span></h4>
<ul>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Prioritize Workload and Programmatic Identities</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Apply the same rigor used for privileged human identities to service principals, service accounts, API keys, cloud access keys, application credentials, deployment tokens, and Personal Access Tokens. These identities should have defined owners, lifecycle controls, least privilege, monitoring, and incident response procedures.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Move From Long-Lived to Short-Lived Credentials</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Remove persistent secrets and use temporary or workload-bound credentials wherever possible. </span></p>
</li>
<ul>
<li style="list-style-type:circle;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Transition from static AWS IAM access keys to temporary credentials through IAM roles.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:circle;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Replace Azure Service Principal client secrets with Managed Identities.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:circle;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Disallow Azure Storage Account Shared Key Authentication in favor of Entra ID authorization with Conditional Access Policies.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:circle;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Use Google Cloud Workload Identity Federation instead of long-lived service-account keys.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:circle;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Move from GitHub classic PATs to fine-grained PATs or workload-based authentication such as GitHub Actions OIDC where supported.</span></p>
</li>
</ul>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Constrain Both the Where and the What</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Limit non-human identities to the APIs, subscriptions, projects, repositories, storage resources, and systems that they actually require. Where supported, restrict the locations and workloads from which those identities can authenticate. A service principal used by an internal CI/CD runner should not also be usable from arbitrary external infrastructure.</span></p>
</li>
<ul>
<li style="list-style-type:circle;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Hardening multi-cloud service principals requires replacing long-lived, static credentials with short-lived tokens and strictly enforcing access controls. Essential approaches across major providers include:</span></p>
</li>
<ul>
<li style="list-style-type:square;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Google Cloud</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Using Workload Identity Federation and targeted IAM constraints.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:square;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">AWS</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Leveraging OIDC federation combined with Service Control Policies (SCPs).</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:square;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Azure</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Applying Conditional Access policies to restrict access contextually.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:square;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Okta</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Implementing Private Key JWT authentication along with network-restricted Authentication Policies.</span></p>
</li>
</ul>
<li style="list-style-type:circle;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Use private connectivity, expected source ranges, workload federation, resource policies, and IAM conditions where appropriate across cloud platforms.</span></p>
</li>
</ul>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Restrict AWS IAM Role Delegation</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Tightly control permissions that allow one identity to assign or pass another identity’s privileges. In AWS, restrict iam:PassRole to specific roles and intended services and apply conditions such as iam:PassedToService where appropriate.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Isolate Credential and Secret Stores</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Place enterprise password vaults and secret management systems behind tightly controlled administrative and network paths. Require strong authentication for privileged access, restrict programmatic access to expected workloads and networks where supported, and alert on unusual bulk secret queries, enumeration, or export activity. These detections overlap with legitimate automation; the signal is in the identity, origin, volume, and sequence, not the action. Baseline known-good automation first and alert on deviation, or expect false positives on routine activity.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Harden Password Reset and Account Lifecycle Processes</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Require strong re-authentication or additional identity verification for high-risk password resets and account recovery. Monitor unusual bursts of password resets, account creation, credential additions, permission changes, or other lifecycle activity.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Automate Secret Hygiene</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Implement continuous scanning of repositories, container images, build artifacts, and pipelines for exposed secrets and credentials. Remove credentials from application code, configuration files, deployment scripts, and databases and move them into dedicated secret-management platforms. Rotate exposed credentials promptly rather than relying on removal from source-control history.</span></p>
</li>
</ul></div></section><section class="Wy08Ac nRhiJb-qJTHM-OWXEXe-hJDwNd nRhiJb-DbgRPb-II5mzb-cGMI2b"><div jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md" jscontroller="M0Q3Qb" data-is-cws-richtext="true" class=" "><h3><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Layer 3: Harden Configurations and Reduce Attack Paths</span></h3>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Remove weaknesses that provide an initial foothold and limit how far a compromised workload can reach.</span></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Automated tooling is particularly effective in environments where several smaller weaknesses can be combined into a larger attack path. An exposed application, an overly privileged workload identity, a credential in an environment variable, and unrestricted network access may each appear manageable in isolation. Together, they can provide a direct path into sensitive systems.</span></p>
<h4><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Why It Is Important</span></h4>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">A vulnerability in an internet-facing application may initially appear isolated. In practice, that application may have access to a backend database. The database may contain credentials. Application environment variables may contain cloud tokens. The underlying host may have an attached cloud identity with access to storage, secrets, or management APIs.</span></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Automation can test those paths in rapid succession. Failures can also become useful feedback. An application error, stack trace, database response, compiler output, or API error may give the tooling enough information to modify its next request within seconds. Detailed diagnostic information exposed externally therefore provides more than troubleshooting value; it can accelerate experimentation during an attack.</span></p>
<h4><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Mitigations</span></h4>
<ul>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Continuously Reduce the External Attack Surface</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Scan internet-facing applications and infrastructure for vulnerabilities, exposed administrative services, default configurations, and unnecessary services. Vulnerability programs should include custom applications, frameworks, libraries, and dependencies in addition to operating systems.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Assume the Workload Can Be Compromised</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Design applications so compromise of the front end provides access only to the systems required for that application’s function. Reduce application permissions and limit access to required databases, APIs, queues, storage accounts, and backend services.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Enforce Application-Tier Segmentation</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Segment internet-facing applications from identity infrastructure, management systems, and unrelated internal services. Restrict DMZ workloads from initiating unnecessary Kerberos, NTLM, LDAP, SMB, RDP, SSH, database, and administrative connections into internal networks.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Harden Database Runtime Privileges</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Application database accounts should not have broad administrative privileges. Restrict capabilities that allow a database engine or application identity to execute operating-system commands, write arbitrary files, modify server-wide settings, or cross the database-to-OS boundary unless the application requires them.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Reduce Credential Exposure in Workloads</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Review application configurations, environment variables, infrastructure-as-code, deployment scripts, CI/CD pipelines, and databases for embedded secrets. Compromise of one workload should not expose credentials for unrelated systems.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Limit Error Feedback to External Users</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Avoid returning detailed stack traces, compiler messages, SQL errors, internal paths, framework details, or other diagnostic information to unauthenticated users. Keep detailed troubleshooting information in internal logs.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Harden Network and Security Appliances</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Remove unnecessary management services from routers, firewalls, VPN appliances, and other infrastructure. Review configurations for unexpected accounts, scheduled jobs, startup scripts, configuration changes, and persistence mechanisms. Validate firmware integrity and vendor signatures where supported.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Harden Active Directory Certificate Services</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Review AD CS for certificate templates and enrollment paths that could allow an attacker to obtain authentication certificates or escalate privileges.</span></p>
</li>
<ul>
<li style="list-style-type:circle;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Remove ENROLLEE_SUPPLIES_SUBJECT where it is not required, particularly on templates supporting client authentication.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:circle;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Restrict enrollment permissions to the identities that require them.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:circle;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Require certificate manager or CA approval for higher-risk enrollment scenarios where appropriate.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:circle;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Enable Extended Protection for Authentication on applicable CA web interfaces where supported.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:circle;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Monitor unusual certificate enrollment involving privileged users or unexpected requesting systems.</span></p>
</li>
</ul>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Reduce Credential Replay and Relay Opportunities</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Enable Windows Credential Guard where appropriate, use Protected Users for suitable privileged accounts, enforce SMB signing, and enforce LDAP signing and channel binding. Reduce or disable NTLM where operationally feasible and continue hardening against credential relay, Pass-the-Hash, Pass-the-Ticket, and related techniques.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Restrict Unnecessary Authentication From Exposed Workloads</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Internet-facing servers should only initiate Kerberos, NTLM, LDAP, SMB, WinRM, or RDP traffic to systems where those protocols are explicitly required.</span></p>
</li>
</ul>
<h3><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Layer 4: Application Protections</span></h3>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Harden legitimate enterprise functionality that attackers can reuse after gaining access.</span></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">A large portion of attacker activity relies on capabilities already present in the environment. Applications, authentication protocols, administrative services, scripting environments, messaging systems, deployment platforms, and business workflows may provide everything needed to continue an intrusion without introducing new malware. Automation makes these capabilities easier to discover and use quickly.</span></p>
<h4><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Why It Is Important</span></h4>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Internal business applications, scripts, and APIs are often customized to each organization without public documentation, and require a level of insider knowledge to understand exploitation opportunities. Automated LLM-powered offensive tooling has enabled attackers to enumerate and rapidly understand these internal applications and use them to their advantage. </span></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Internal communication functionality is one example. A compromised application account may legitimately have access to a notification, messaging, or email API. Sending messages is expected behavior for that application. That same capability can be abused to send hundreds or thousands of phishing messages. Without recipient limits, rate limits, or behavioral monitoring, a standard business function can become a high-speed distribution mechanism.</span></p>
<h4><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Mitigations</span></h4>
<ul>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Put Guardrails Around High-Impact Business APIs</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Applications capable of accessing sensitive data, retrieving credentials, sending email, notifications, creating users, modifying access, or performing other high-impact actions should have rate limits, recipient limits, transaction thresholds, and abuse controls appropriate to their function. Alert when an application operates materially outside its normal volume.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Inventory Legitimate Administrative Capabilities Before an Incident:</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> Security teams should know which platforms provide remote command execution, deployment, scripting, certificate issuance, identity synchronization, bulk messaging, and other high-impact functions. Those capabilities should have appropriate restrictions and monitoring in place before they are used during an intrusion.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Conduct Automated Application Testing and Threat Modelling</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: All internal applications should have SAST and DAST scans conducted and findings resolved prior to deployment. Organizations can also leverage LLM-enabled tooling to automate the discovery, triage, reproduction, and patching of software vulnerabilities and potential attack paths. Organizations can also apply agentic source-code review and continuous AI-assisted vulnerability testing—whether a bespoke LLM harness or a managed capability such as </span><a href="https://cloud.google.com/security/ai-threat-defense"><span style="text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline">Google AI Threat Defense</span></a><span style="vertical-align:baseline">—to find, triage, and reproduce these attack paths before an attacker does.</span></p>
</li>
</ul></div></section><section class="Wy08Ac nRhiJb-qJTHM-OWXEXe-hJDwNd nRhiJb-DbgRPb-II5mzb-cGMI2b"><div jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md" jscontroller="M0Q3Qb" data-is-cws-richtext="true" class=" "><h3><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Layer 5: Detect Behavioral and Volumetric Change</span></h3>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Detect when identities, applications, workloads, and infrastructure begin operating outside their established patterns.</span></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Many actions performed during an automated attack are legitimate operations when viewed individually. Detection therefore depends heavily on context: identity, source network, volume, timing, sequence, target resource, and historical activity.</span></p>
<h4><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Why It Is Important</span></h4>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">A service principal accessing cloud storage is not necessarily suspicious. A service principal that normally accesses ten objects from one internal network and suddenly downloads thousands of objects from an unfamiliar hosting provider is a different situation.</span></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Outbound activity provides similar context. Automated tooling on a compromised server may contact external webhook services to confirm execution or transfer results. It may also communicate with LLM platforms, temporary file-sharing infrastructure, tunneling services, or attacker-controlled systems.</span></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">A single HTTPS connection may be routine. A production server that historically communicates with a small set of known destinations and suddenly begins reaching several new external services deserves closer attention.</span></p>
<h4><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Mitigations and Detection Priorities</span></h4>
<ul>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Baseline Behavior at the Identity and Workload Level</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Establish expected authentication sources, APIs, resources, activity volumes, and communication patterns for sensitive service principals, applications, workloads, and administrative identities.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Detect Changes in Authentication Origin</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Track the networks, ASNs, cloud regions, hosting providers, and other infrastructure used by privileged users and workload identities. Investigate successful authentication from infrastructure that falls outside the identity’s normal pattern.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Monitor Control-Plane Velocity</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Alert on unusual bursts of management API calls, permission testing, configuration changes, resource enumeration, credential retrieval, and other administrative activity. The timing and sequence of legitimate API calls can provide important context.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Baseline Data-Plane Activity</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Establish normal read, write, and object-access patterns for sensitive storage and data platforms. Detect sudden enumeration, unusual write-read-delete sequences, large increases in object access, or material changes in bytes transferred.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Build Detections Around Sequences</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Correlate related events across identity, control-plane, data-plane, endpoint, and network telemetry. A new authentication source followed by permission probing, storage enumeration, bulk downloads, and a connection to a previously unseen external service should be analyzed as one sequence.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Monitor East-West Behavior From Exposed Workloads</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Detect unexpected LDAP, Kerberos, NTLM, SMB, database, remote administration, and port-scanning activity originating from DMZ or internet-facing systems.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Control and Monitor Server Egress</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Restrict unnecessary outbound internet connectivity from production servers and route sensitive workloads through controlled egress paths where practical. Monitor previously unseen destinations, particularly webhook services, OAST infrastructure, tunneling platforms, public file-transfer services, and external AI platforms that are not required by the workload.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Preserve the Telemetry Needed to See the Behavior</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Collect and centralize interactive and non-interactive identity logs, service-principal authentication, cloud control-plane activity, sensitive storage data-plane logs, application administration logs, network flow, DNS, proxy telemetry, and endpoint activity. Establishing normal behavior depends on having enough historical data to compare against current activity.</span></p>
</li>
</ul>
<h3><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Google Security Operations (SecOps)</span></h3>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Google SecOps customers have access to these broad category rules and more under the Mandiant Frontline Threats and Mandiant Hunting Rules rule pack. The activity discussed in the blog post is detected in Google SecOps under the rule names:</span></p>
<ul>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Suspicious Webhook.Site Interaction</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Suspicious Curl Invocation</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Cmd Launching Curl</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">AWS Cloud Accounts Getcalleridentity</span></p>
</li>
</ul></div></section><section class="kcBhad"><section class="Fabbec"><span class="WrMNjb">Posted in</span><ul class="FzXI4e"><li class="I4B51b"><a Threat Intelligence Group "],["Mandiant "]],[null,"\u003cscript type\u003d\"application/ld+json\"\u003e{\"@context\":\"https://schema.org\",\"@type\":\"BlogPosting\",\"@id\":\"https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/artex-and-friends-enter-agentic-offensive-frameworks\",\"headline\":\"ARTEX and Friends: Enter Agentic Offensive Frameworks\",\"description\":\"Mandiant and GTIG provide remediation guidance against a new paradigm of machine-speed intrusions driven by threat actors using ARTEX and similar agentic pentesting frameworks.\",\"image\":\"https://storage.googleapis.com/gweb-cloudblog-publish/images/03_ThreatIntelligenceWebsiteBannerIdeas_BA.max-2600x2600.png\",\"author\":[{\"@type\":\"Person\",\"name\":\"Google Threat Intelligence Group \",\"url\":\"\"},{\"@type\":\"Person\",\"name\":\"Mandiant \",\"url\":\"\"}],\"datePublished\":\"2026-10-30\",\"publisher\":{\"@type\":\"Organization\",\"name\":\"Google Cloud\",\"logo\":{\"@type\":\"ImageObject\",\"url\":\"https://www.gstatic.com/cgc/google-cloud-logo.svg\"}},\"url\":\"https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/artex-and-friends-enter-agentic-offensive-frameworks\",\"keywords\":[\"Threat Intelligence\"],\"timeRequired\":\"PT35M\"}\u003c/script\u003e"],["ARTEX and Friends: Enter Agentic Offensive Frameworks"],null,null,[[null,null,[null,[null,"\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eWritten by: Aurora Blum, David Mainor, Rohit Nambiar, Omar ElAhdan, Matthew McWhirt, Will Silverstone, Bhavesh Dhake, Chris Busse\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003chr\u003e\n\u003ch3\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eIntroduction\u003c/span\u003e\u003cstrong style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e \u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/h3\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eRecent intrusions in which threat actors have been observed weaponizing agentic frameworks to execute highly-automated intrusions has put a spotlight on evolving adversarial use of LLMs. In our \u003c/span\u003e\u003ca href\u003d\"https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/from-prompting-to-autonomy-the-evolution-of-adversarial-ai\"\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eSeptember 2026 report\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/a\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e, Google Threat Intelligence Group (GTIG) noted that forward leaning adversaries were beginning to transition from basic prompting to agentic workflows and LLM-enabled automation. \u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eSince this publication, GTIG and Mandiant have observed multiple threat actors abusing the open-source, agentic pentesting frameworks ARTEX, Hexstrike and CAIRN in incident response engagements to conduct a broad range of actions across multiple phases of the attack life cycle. This activity has been seen across the threat landscape, as noted in a recent Wiz \u003c/span\u003e\u003ca href\u003d\"https://www.wiz.io/blog/artex-in-the-wild-storm-0940-expands-targeting-of-it-and-software-vendors\" rel\u003d\"noopener\" target\u003d\"_blank\"\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003ereport\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/a\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e. The emergence of Artex and similar agentic offensive intrusion frameworks underscores a broader shift toward threat actor adoption of open source multi-agent offensive frameworks, as well as potentially custom agentic harnesses.  \u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eThis represents a paradigm shift towards intrusions occurring at machine speed, with the automated chaining of complex attack paths scaling intrusions exponentially. While the mechanics of the intrusions remain the same, such as using compromised credentials and targeting unpatched systems, the speed and scale at which frameworks can exploit these known weaknesses introduces a new defensive challenge. Security teams, developers, and system administrators will need to fundamentally rethink defense strategies to counter scalable, autonomous software. \u003c/span\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eWe also provide defensive strategies from Mandiant Consulting. The defensive strategies suggested—control-plane isolation, short-lived credentials, least privilege, segmentation, and behavioral detection—are long-standing security best practices. \u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003ch3\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eObservations in the Wild\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/h3\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eWe are seeing tools used by adversaries evolving towards autonomous agentic attacks. One way this is occurring is through suites of tools or frameworks designed for penetration testing (“pentesting”). These tools and frameworks orchestrate various agents using open source and frontier LLMs to automate various stages of intrusions. Historically, cyber espionage and financially motivated threat actors have taken advantage of pentesting or red-teaming platforms built for security professionals to exploit systems, such as Cobalt Strike or Metasploit. Today, adversaries are pivoting toward offensive frameworks powered by LLMs. While these frameworks have existed in the wild, they are now yielding success in intrusions in the wild, representing a critical evolution that increases both the speed and scale of intrusion activity. \u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eSince late August 2026, we have observed threat actors actively operating popular open-source multi-agent pentesting platforms like ARTEX, PentAGI, CAIRN and other potentially custom harnesses within Mandiant incident response engagements; use of these platforms has been further corroborated by our research telemetry. In these incidents\u003c/span\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e, we have observed agentic activity conducting significant parts of the attack lifecycle (e.g., lateral movement, database exploitation, exfiltration preparation). Evidence of in the wild usage of these frameworks marks a critical inflection point for defenders, as agents now autonomously explore multiple attack paths, troubleshoot errors, and achieve objectives in seconds and minutes. Coupled with continual advancements in underlying LLM capabilities, the acceleration in attack velocity and scale signals a notable shift in the threat landscape that fundamentally alters traditional intrusion dynamics.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e"]]],[null,null,[null,[null,"\u003ch4\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eARTEX Framework\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/h4\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eARTEX is an autonomous, multi-agent LLM-driven pentesting and offensive command-and-control (C2) platform written in Go with an embedded Next.js web console. The framework was originally developed by the user \u003c/span\u003e\u003ca href\u003d\"https://github.com/Autumn-27\" rel\u003d\"noopener\" target\u003d\"_blank\"\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eAutumn-27\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/a\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e and released on GitHub. Although the original repository was taken down by its creator in October 2026 due to widespread threat actor abuse, the framework continues to proliferate globally via community forks, including localized variations such as ScopeWeaver and a commercial version dubbed ARTEX PRO. ARTEX abstracts the attack lifecycle across six specialized sub-agents. By leveraging APIs from frontier and open source models, the framework translates high-level tactical goals into actionable machine commands. GTIG has observed capabilities including automated Active Directory reconnaissance, privilege escalation, target cloud-infrastructure profiling (such as harvesting Azure storage keys), to the synthesis and deployment of custom webshells.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eSince late August 2026, ARTEX has been adopted by a diverse range of threat actors across the motivational spectrum, including both financially motivated extortion groups and suspected cyber espionage operators. For example, the Wiz Research team has been tracking a campaign associated with ARTEX, which GTIG tracks as UNC7228, that overlaps with activity tracked by \u003c/span\u003e\u003ca href\u003d\"https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/security/blog/2026/10/21/storm-0940-uses-artex-agentic-ai-connectwise-supply-chain-compromise/\" rel\u003d\"noopener\" target\u003d\"_blank\"\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eMicrosoft as the PRC-nexus actor STORM-0940\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/a\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e. The research has expanded Wiz’s understanding of this campaign beyond the \u003c/span\u003e\u003ca href\u003d\"https://www.wiz.io/blog/artex-in-the-wild-storm-0940-expands-targeting-of-it-and-software-vendors\" rel\u003d\"noopener\" target\u003d\"_blank\"\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eOctober IT focused activity\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/a\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e with activity now dating back to at least August 2026. Wiz identified the targeting, compromise, and data theft affecting multiple entities in the US and Europe across the enterprise software, industrial automation, semiconductor manufacturing and design, and telecommunications industry verticals. This actor has moved extremely quickly, typically moving from their first compromised account to deep system access in around four hours and in one case as rapidly as 15 minutes. They have also customized their tooling to take advantage of victim-specific setups and fallen back to open proxies if their initial infrastructure was blocked.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eDemonstrating broader adoption of ARTEX, GTIG and Mandiant observed multiple other threat actors using ARTEX for post-compromise reconnaissance, lateral movement, and privilege escalation in incident response investigations. This includes financially motivated threat actors who used the framework to facilitate data theft extortion, and additional suspected PRC-nexus clusters. Their operations collectively impacted organizations across government, technology, chemicals and materials, and manufacturing sectors in the US, alongside \u003c/span\u003e\u003ca href\u003d\"https://www.crowdstrike.com/en-us/blog/unknown-threat-actor-uses-artex-to-target-south-korean-finance/\" rel\u003d\"noopener\" target\u003d\"_blank\"\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003epublic reporting\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/a\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e of ARTEX targeting the financial sector in Asia. \u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eIn several cases, adversaries provided ARTEX with compromised credentials for the targeted organization, and instructed the framework to execute subsequent stages of the attack lifecycle. Analysis of these intrusions reveals key differences between activity conducted by the ARTEX framework and that of a human operator:\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cul\u003e\n\u003cli style\u003d\"list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eBased on log files and timestamps, we identified bursts of activity that would be highly irregular for a human operator. While each of the intrusions exhibited extended pauses during operational phases, individual ARTEX execution sequences ran in compressed bursts of roughly 2 to 25 seconds. In at least one operation, we observed 27 events in 23 seconds of active burst time, performing rapid probing of AWS permissions across different security groups. While preparing potential exfiltration scripts, ARTEX was capable of successfully adapting to errors quoting “script too large” by trimming the script and relaunching it to successfully deploy a 17-snapshot instance in approximately 10 seconds. \u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli style\u003d\"list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eCompared to a competent human operator, ARTEX achieved code execution in a far more forensically noisy manner, relying on rapid, iterative trial-and-error. In one instance, supplied with valid credentials, ARTEX accessed an external-facing Linux Weaver OA web server to execute SQL commands via exposed API calls. Using legitimately-exposed application-specific API calls, ARTEX was able to achieve partial command-execution to conduct further reconnaissance despite hitting technical complexities. However, this process generated 240 log events and exhibited errors potentially caused by multiple sub-agents overwriting one another while pursuing the same objective. \u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cul\u003e\n\u003cli style\u003d\"list-style-type:circle;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eIn an attempt to pivot from a database-execution context to a system-execution context, ARTEX used an application-specific SQL API, accessible to the supplied-user credential, to modify the MySQL instance to write log files with a\u003c/span\u003e\u003ccode style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e .jsp\u003c/code\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e extension to publicly accessible locations. The SQL-commands executed were likely intended to poison MySQL logs into writing web-accessible JSP webshells.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli style\u003d\"list-style-type:circle;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eUsing the API, ARTEX ran \u003c/span\u003e\u003ccode style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eSET GLOBAL general_log_file \u003d \u0026#39;\u0026lt;path to webshell\u0026gt;’ \u003c/code\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003efollowed by\u003c/span\u003e\u003ccode style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e SET GLOBAL general_log \u003d 1\u003c/code\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e; subsequent SQL commands were written to the filepath defined in \u003c/span\u003e\u003ccode style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003egeneral_log_file\u003c/code\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e. ARTEX then ran SQL SELECT statements in the form\u003c/span\u003e\u003ccode style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e `SELECT \u0026#39;\u0026lt;% [webshell content] %\u0026gt;\u0026#39;`\u003c/code\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e. Due to how the underlying Java web servlet handles JSP files, the Java code in the \u0026#39;\u0026lt;% %\u0026gt;\u0026#39; gets parsed and compiled into the process memory space, providing means of execution.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli style\u003d\"list-style-type:circle;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eLog activity shows multiple iterations of the above sequence, with ephemeral, somewhat-incremental file names (\u003c/span\u003e\u003ccode style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003ecl1217.jsp, f1219a.jsp, z1219.jsp\u003c/code\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e) for each webshell candidate, each one executing a different command. Errors when trying to run the webshell (e.g.\u003c/span\u003e\u003ccode style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e variable ‘t is already defined\u003c/code\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e’) caused the webshell to fail and likely had ARTEX refactor its approach, writing several unique webshells, each with a different goal, some correcting previous errors until the\u003c/span\u003e\u003ccode style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e .jsp\u003c/code\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e webshells executed cleanly and returned valid command output to ARTEX.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003c/ul\u003e\n\u003c/ul\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eIn another case, during access-probing efforts, ARTEX generated files of various extension types with the filename \u003c/span\u003e\u003ccode style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eartex-probe-*\u003c/code\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e, where\u003c/span\u003e\u003ccode style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e * \u003c/code\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003ecarried five digit somewhat-incremental numbers. Based on our visibility, it’s difficult to comment on the exact significance of these numbers or how they increment. However, ARTEX was seen using similar incremental numeric markers within dropped file names, created account names, their passwords and within email subjects. \u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003ch4\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"font-style:italic;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eObserved ARTEX Activity Mapped along the phases of a targeted attack lifecycle \u003c/span\u003e\u003c/h4\u003e"]]],[null,null,[null,[null,"\u003cdiv align\u003d\"left\"\u003e\n\u003cdiv
